People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Hi, Doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me. Um... I hope I'm not taking up too much of your time. 

I came in mostly because... well, my husband and the people at the warehouse where I work, they've been kind of... acting like I'm crazy. Which I'm not, I promise. It's just... well, I know they've been watching my moves on the floor. And they keep changing my pallet counts. I'm pretty sure they're just doing it to mess with my numbers so they can keep me from getting that supervisor spot.

Patient reports beliefs about coworkers watching her and deliberately sabotaging her work (potential ideas of reference/persecution). Defensive tone ('I'm not crazy, I promise') and minimization ('people get competitive') warrant exploration of paranoid ideation, delusional thinking, or personality pathology. F22 (Delusional disorder), F23 (Brief psychotic disorder), F21 (Schizotypal disorder), F60.0 (Paranoid personality disorder), and F60.1 (Schizoid personality disorder) are initial differentials pending further assessment of symptom duration, pervasiveness, functional impact, and reality testing.",

Um... well... I really don't want to sound difficult, and I appreciate you trying to help me figure all this out. But... I do think it's real.

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... I really wish I could tell you yes. I honestly do, because I know that's what you're hoping I'll say, and I really want to do this right for you. I don't want to be a difficult case or make you feel like I'm not trying. 

But... no. Even late at night, I don't really doubt it. I know I keep repeating myself, and I'm so sorry if it's getting annoying, but... like I said, the only reason I even agreed to come in here is just because my husband and the warehouse guys keep acting like I'm crazy. And I hate it. I just want them to be okay with me again and see that I'm good at my job. 

But I can't just pretend I don't see what's happening. I *know* they've been watching my moves. I know they're changing those pallet counts behind my back to keep me from getting that supervisor spot. I see it happening, so I can't really doubt it, even when I'm all alone. 

I'm really sorry... I know that means I'm not giving you the answer you were looking for. Are you mad at me for saying that? I'm just trying to be completely honest with you like you asked... is that okay?

I'm really just trying to do a good job and mind my own business.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing that people at work were watching you and changing your pallet counts?\n\nPatient: Um... well, it's been going on for a little while now. I'd say maybe... about eight months? Give or take a little? \n\nI hope it doesn't sound too strange, but along with the feeling that they're watching me and messing with my counts, I've also been... well, hearing some voices, too. Just for about that same amount of time, the last eight months or so. I know that probably sounds bad, and I really didn't want to bring it up because I don't want you to think I'm, you know, unstable or anything. It's just been a lot to deal with. Does that... does that seem like a long time to you? I should have come in sooner, shouldn't I?\n\nDoctor: When you hear these voices, what do they say to you? Do they give you commands or instructions, or do they comment on what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Um... well, they aren't mean or anything. I don't want you to think I'm hearing, like, scary things or dangerous things. It's actually pretty normal-sounding. \n\nSometimes, like if I'm in the aisle at the warehouse, or even just at home folding clothes, I'll hear this man's voice. And it's clear as day, just like someone standing right next to me. He just says, \"Nicole, check it again.\" Even when I know nobody is anywhere near me. \n\nIt's just a little reminder, you know? Not a big deal or anything. It's not like he's telling me to do bad things. That's... that's not too crazy, right? I mean, maybe my brain is just trying to help me be better at my job? It's... it's just directed at me. Like I said, it's just that one thing, \"Nicole, check it again.\" \n\nAnd it's really just... well, like I mentioned, maybe when I'm in the aisle at work, or just doing something simple at home, like folding clothes. It's just a man's voice, and it sounds perfectly clear, like he's right there, even when I'm completely alone. \n\nBut it's just to me, you know? Not a conversation about me. I hope that makes sense. It's really not as bad as it sounds, I promise. That's... that's an okay answer, right? I'm trying to explain it the best I can.\n\nDoctor: Nicole, looking back on everything you've described — the voices, the feeling that people are watching you and changing your pallet counts — do you think these experiences might be signs of something medical or psychiatric going on, or do you believe they're definitely real things actually happening around you?\n\nPatient: Um... well... I really don't want to sound difficult, and I appreciate you trying to help me figure all this out. But... I do think it's real. \n\nHonestly, the main reason I even came in to see you is because my husband and the warehouse people keep acting like I'm... well, like I'm crazy. I just hope you don't think I'm completely losing my mind for saying it.\n\nDoctor: Nicole, I want to ask you something directly. If I told you that what you're describing — the voices and the feeling that people are coordinating to watch and change your counts — could be caused by your brain working differently right now, not by actual people doing these things, would that be something you could consider as a possibility?\n\nPatient: Um... It really feels like they're doing it to keep me from getting that supervisor spot. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that's not what you were hoping I'd say.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um... I really, really wish I could just say yes. I know that's what a good patient would say, and I want to agree with you so badly because you've been so nice and patient with me today. I don't want you to think I'm just being difficult on purpose or ignoring your professional opinion. 

But... I just don't feel like my mind is playing tricks on me. I know I'm saying the exact same thing again, and I'm so embarrassed to keep repeating myself like this. I'm sorry. It's just... the whole reason I came in is because my husband and the people at the warehouse keep acting like I'm crazy. And I hate that so much. But I just know they've been watching my moves. I know they're changing those pallet counts to keep me from getting that supervisor spot. 

It doesn't feel like my brain is making it up, it just feels like it's really happening to me, clear as day. I'm so sorry... I feel like I'm doing a bad job answering your questions by not agreeing with you. Am I doing this wrong? I really hope you aren't mad at me for sticking to my guns on this.

I'm trying to explain it the best I can.\n\nDoctor: Nicole, looking back on everything you've described — the voices, the feeling that people are watching you and changing your pallet counts — do you think these experiences might be signs of something medical or psychiatric going on, or do you believe they're definitely real things actually happening around you?\n\nPatient: Um... well... I really don't want to sound difficult, and I appreciate you trying to help me figure all this out. But... I do think it's real. \n\nHonestly, the main reason I even came in to see you is because my husband and the warehouse people keep acting like I'm... well, like I'm crazy. And I really hate that they think that about me. I don't want to be a problem for anybody, I really don't. \n\nBut I just know they have been watching my moves and changing the pallet counts on me. There's this supervisor spot, and... well, I really think they're doing it to keep me from getting that spot. I'm sorry, I know that probably sounds like I'm just making excuses or being paranoid, doesn't it? But it's what's actually happening. Is it okay that I feel that way? I just hope you don't think I'm completely losing my mind for saying it.\n\nDoctor: Nicole, I want to ask you something directly. If I told you that what you're describing — the voices and the feeling that people are coordinating to watch and change your counts — could be caused by your brain working differently right now, not by actual people doing these things, would that be something you could consider as a possibility?\n\nPatient: Um... I mean, I guess I can see why you would say that. I really do try to keep an open mind, and I know you're the doctor and you know about these things. I don't want to be stubborn or anything. \n\nBut... it's just really hard for me to believe it's just my brain. Because, like I said, I only really came in because my husband and the people at work keep acting like I'm crazy. And I hate that. I really want them to be happy with me. But I just know they've been watching my moves and changing those pallet counts. It really feels like they're doing it to keep me from getting that supervisor spot. \n\nI'm sorry, I know that's not what you were hoping I'd say. I really don't want to disappoint you or make your job harder. I know it sounds like I'm not listening to you, but it just feels so real to me. Is... is it okay that I still feel that way? I'm not making you upset by saying that, am I?\n\nDoctor: Nicole, aside from coming to see me today, have you been able to keep doing your job at the warehouse and handle things at home the same way you did before the voices and the watching started eight months ago? Or have these experiences made it harder for you to do your work or take care of things?\n\nPatient: Um... well, I've still been going to work every day. Is that... is that the kind of information you needed?\n\nDoctor: Nicole, if I were to tell you that the voices and the watching are not actually happening — that they're experiences your brain is creating — would you be able to accept that as true, or do you feel absolutely certain that these things are really happening around you?\n\nPatient: Um... well... I know I'm probably repeating myself, and I really, really don't want to sound like I'm arguing with you. I know you're just trying to help me, and I appreciate that so much. But... it's just so hard to accept that it's all in my head. \n\nI mean, the whole reason I'm even sitting here today is just because my husband and everyone at the warehouse keep treating me like I'm... like I'm crazy. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.
